========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Yeah, so, I mean, I made this appointment because... well, it's just getting to be way too much to handle. I'm in college right now, doing a radiologic technologist program, which is great, I really like the clinical side of it, but... I came in because I feel like I’m literally crawling out of my skin whenever I’m supposed to just sit still. 

It happens all the time in the radiology suite if we're between patients, or especially in the classroom during long lectures. I've been trying to just tough it out for months, you know? Just telling myself to sit in the chair, focus, and be normal like everyone else. I really try to just clamp down on it. But it never works. I always, always end up pacing around the back of the room, or shifting constantly in my seat, or just finding some completely random excuse to get up and move. Like, I'll pretend I need to check the lead aprons or go to the bathroom for the third time just so I can walk down the hall and shake it off. It’s like this intense physical pressure building up in my legs, and my supervisors are definitely starting to notice that I just can't stay put, so I figured I finally needed to come talk to somebody about it.

No, no, I'm not taking anything at all. Like, literally nothing. I don't even really take over-the-counter stuff, like Tylenol or Advil or whatever, unless I absolutely have to. Which is pretty rare, maybe if I get a headache from staring at the x-ray monitors in the dark room for too long? But even then I usually just try to drink water or, you know, walk it off. 

I definitely don't take any psychiatric medications, never have. I've never actually seen a doctor for any of this kind of stuff before today, so no one's ever prescribed me anything. And anti-nausea stuff? No, my stomach is usually totally fine, thankfully. Because working in the hospital you see a lot of gross stuff and some people in my cohort get totally green, but I've got an iron stomach, so I never need Pepto or anything like that. I don't even take vitamins, honestly. I mean, my mom bought me these gummy vitamins when I started the program but I think they're just sitting on my desk getting stale because I always, always forget to take them anyway. So yeah, literally nothing in my system, no prescriptions, no pharmacy stuff, just... just me. But no pills.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's really not like a worrying thing at all. I mean, it's not like my brain is spinning about, you know, failing a test or messing up a scan or anything like that. It's so weird to explain, but it's completely physical. Like, I don't feel anxious in my head, I just feel this... this crazy energy in my body. It's like my muscles are humming or vibrating, especially my legs, and if I don't move them, it literally feels like they're going to snap or something. 

Like the other day, I was sitting in this lecture about radiation physics, which is actually super interesting, I really like learning about how the x-ray tubes work and the anode heel effect and all that stuff. But I was sitting there, and I wasn't worried about the material or my grades or anything, I was actually trying really hard to pay attention. But my legs just started doing this bouncing thing, like my heel was just going up and down, up and down, a million miles an hour. And I was trying to press my hands down on my knees to stop it, but the pressure just kept building up in my chest and my arms and my legs until I felt like I was going to explode if I didn't get up. So I ended up having to get up and go to the back of the room and just stand there and kind of shift my weight from foot to foot for the rest of the class. And the whole time, my mind was totally fine, I wasn't stressing about anything, I just physically could not keep my body in that chair. It's just this intense, overwhelming urge to be doing something, anything, with my body. It's never about being scared or worried or having anxious thoughts, it's just... I just have to move. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just a physical need, you know?

I mean, it's not like my brain is spinning about, you know, failing a test or messing up a scan or anything like that. It's so weird to explain, but it's completely physical. Like, I don't feel anxious in my head, I just feel this... this crazy energy in my body. It's like my muscles are humming or vibrating, especially my legs, and if I don't move them, it literally feels like they're going to snap or something. \n\nLike the other day, I was sitting in this lecture about radiation physics, which is actually super interesting, I really like learning about how the x-ray tubes work and the anode heel effect and all that stuff. But I was sitting there, and I wasn't worried about the material or my grades or anything, I was actually trying really hard to pay attention. But my legs just started doing this bouncing thing, like my heel was just going up and down, up and down, a million miles an hour. And I was trying to press my hands down on my knees to stop it, but the pressure just kept building up in my chest and my arms and my legs until I felt like I was going to explode if I didn't get up. So I ended up having to get up and go to the back of the room and just stand there and kind of shift my weight from foot to foot for the rest of the class. And the whole time, my mind was totally fine, I wasn't stressing about anything, I just physically could not keep my body in that chair. It's just this intense, overwhelming urge to be doing something, anything, with my body. It's never about being scared or worried or having anxious thoughts, it's just... I just have to move. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just a physical need, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, wow, no, it's definitely not a new thing. Not at all. I mean, my parents still bring it up all the time, especially when we have family dinners or whatever. They love to tell stories about my elementary school parent-teacher conferences, which, honestly, were always super embarrassing. Like, they'd sit down with Mrs. Davis in second grade or Mr. Henderson in fourth grade, and it was always the exact same speech. The teachers would be like, "He's a smart kid, but he just cannot stay in his seat." 

I guess I was always popping up to sharpen my pencil, or go to the trash can, or just stand in the back of the room for literally no reason. And they said I was always calling out answers before the teacher even finished asking the question, or I'd just start the worksheet before they even handed out the instructions. Like, I just couldn't wait. I remember always feeling like everything was moving in slow motion and I just needed to speed it up. So yeah, this has been going on way, way before middle school, and definitely way before I started the radiologic technologist program. It's just that now, in college, the stakes are higher, and I can't just get away with being the "energetic kid" anymore. But yeah, this restless, gotta-move feeling? It's basically been the story of my life since I was little. It's always been there.

I've never actually seen a doctor for any of this kind of stuff before today, so no one's ever prescribed me anything. And anti-nausea stuff? No, my stomach is usually totally fine, thankfully. Because working in the hospital you see a lot of gross stuff and some people in my cohort get totally green, but I've got an iron stomach, so I never need Pepto or anything like that. I don't even take vitamins, honestly. I mean, my mom bought me these gummy vitamins when I started the program but I think they're just sitting on my desk getting stale because I always, always forget to take them anyway. So yeah, literally nothing in my system, no prescriptions, no pharmacy stuff, just... just me. But no pills.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move, do you also notice yourself worrying or having anxious thoughts running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical feeling of needing to move without particular worries attached?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not like a worrying thing at all. I mean, it's not like my brain is spinning about, you know, failing a test or messing up a scan or anything like that. It's so weird to explain, but it's completely physical. Like, I don't feel anxious in my head, I just feel this... this crazy energy in my body. It's like my muscles are humming or vibrating, especially my legs, and if I don't move them, it literally feels like they're going to snap or something. \n\nLike the other day, I was sitting in this lecture about radiation physics, which is actually super interesting, I really like learning about how the x-ray tubes work and the anode heel effect and all that stuff. But I was sitting there, and I wasn't worried about the material or my grades or anything, I was actually trying really hard to pay attention. But my legs just started doing this bouncing thing, like my heel was just going up and down, up and down, a million miles an hour. And I was trying to press my hands down on my knees to stop it, but the pressure just kept building up in my chest and my arms and my legs until I felt like I was going to explode if I didn't get up. It's just this intense, overwhelming urge to be doing something, anything, with my body. It's never about being scared or worried or having anxious thoughts, it's just... I just have to move. Honestly, no, not really. It's weird because people always kind of assume that, right? Like, if you're bouncing off the walls or you can't stay in your seat, you must be zoning out or forgetting what you're supposed to be doing. But for me, it's really not like that at all. My memory is actually super sharp, and I don't lose track of things. 

Like, in my clinicals, there are a ton of steps you have to remember for every single exam, and I never forget them. Take a standard PA chest x-ray, for example. I know exactly what to do, every single time, in the exact right order. I bring the patient in, verify their name and date of birth—two patient identifiers, always, they drill that into us—then I get them up against the wall bucky, make sure they're centered, roll their shoulders forward so the scapulae are out of the lung fields, tell them to take a deep breath in, blow it out, take another deep breath and hold it... I remember every single one of those steps perfectly. I never skip a beat or forget to set my kVp and mAs on the control panel. I know some of the other students in my cohort have to carry around these little pocket guidebooks because they forget the exact centering points or the angulation of the tube for different joints, but I don't need that. I just know it. I can focus on the task totally fine. It's just that while I'm doing it, I'm practically jogging in place, or I'm rushing through the steps so fast because I just want to keep moving to the next thing. 

And with homework or assignments, I definitely don't forget them. If anything, the second a professor posts an assignment online or hands out a worksheet, I just do it immediately, right there in the lecture hall, just so I don't have to sit down and force myself to do it later. I just power through it as fast as humanly possible. 

So, yeah, no, my focus is honestly completely fine, and I keep track of everything I need

I bring the patient in, verify their name and date of birth—two patient identifiers, always, they drill that into us—then I get them up against the wall bucky, make sure they're centered, roll their shoulders forward so the scapulae are out of the lung fields, tell them to take a deep breath in, blow it out, take another deep breath and hold it... I remember every single one of those steps perfectly. I never skip a beat or forget to set my kVp and mAs on the control panel. I know some of the other students in my cohort have to carry around these little pocket guidebooks because they forget the exact centering points or the angulation of the tube for different joints, but I don't need that. I just know it. I can focus on the task totally fine. It's just that while I'm doing it, I'm practically jogging in place, or I'm rushing through the steps so fast because I just want to keep moving to the next thing. \n\nAnd with homework or assignments, I definitely don't forget them. If anything, the second a professor posts an assignment online or hands out a worksheet, I just do it immediately, right there in the lecture hall, just so I don't have to sit down and force myself to do it later. I just power through it as fast as humanly possible. \n\nSo, yeah, no, my focus is honestly completely fine, and I keep track of everything I need\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, yeah. Yeah, definitely. It's actually a huge problem for me, especially now in my clinical rotations. Like, I just... I can't seem to hold it in. It's like the words are just right there on the tip of my tongue, pushing against my teeth, and I just have to spit them out. 

It happens all the time with my clinical supervisors. They'll be going over something, right? Like, say we're doing a lateral knee, and the supervisor starts to explain the tube angulation, and they'll be like, "Okay, so for this patient, because of their pelvis size, we're going to angle the tube..." and before they can even finish the sentence, I'll just blurt out, "Five degrees cephalad!" Because I know it, right? I know the textbook answer. But then they'll look at me and be like, "Well, actually, I was going to say we need to angle it differently because this specific patient has a suspected fracture and we can't position them the standard way." And then I just feel like an idiot because I jumped the gun and totally missed the critical piece of information that actually mattered for that specific scenario. 

It's just... I get so eager, or maybe impatient is a better word? Like, my brain just wants to get to the end of the interaction. I know where the sentence is going, or at least I think I do, so I just finish it for them. I do it with my friends, too. They'll be telling a story, and I'll just keep interrupting to guess what happens next or to jump to the punchline. It's not that I'm trying to be rude or a know-it-all, I swear. It's just this impulsive... reflex, almost. Like my mouth moves faster than my brain can tell it to wait. And it's so frustrating because I know it annoys people. My supervisors have definitely gotten onto me about it. They're always telling me, "Just listen, let me finish, take a breath." But in the moment, it just slips out before I can even catch myself. It's like I have zero filter between having a thought and saying it out loud.

I mean, it's not like my brain is spinning about, you know, failing a test or messing up a scan or anything like that. It's so weird to explain, but it's completely physical. Like, I don't feel anxious in my head, I just feel this... this crazy energy in my body. It's like my muscles are humming or vibrating, especially my legs, and if I don't move them, it literally feels like they're going to snap or something. \n\nLike the other day, I was sitting in this lecture about radiation physics, which is actually super interesting, I really like learning about how the x-ray tubes work and the anode heel effect and all that stuff. But I was sitting there, and I wasn't worried about the material or my grades or anything, I was actually trying really hard to pay attention. But my legs just started doing this bouncing thing, like my heel was just going up and down, up and down, a million miles an hour. And I was trying to press my hands down on my knees to stop it, but the pressure just kept building up in my chest and my arms and my legs until I felt like I was going to explode if I didn't get up. So I ended up having to get up and go to the back of the room and just stand there and kind of shift my weight from foot to foot for the rest of the class. And the whole time, my mind was totally fine, I wasn't stressing about anything, I just physically could not keep my body in that chair. It's just this intense, overwhelming urge to be doing something, anything, with my body. It's never about being scared or worried or having anxious thoughts, it's just... I just have to move. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just a physical need, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience this kind of restlessness or difficulty sitting still back in childhood or grade school, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, no, it's definitely not a new thing. Not at all. I mean, my parents still bring it up all the time, especially when we have family dinners or whatever. They love to tell stories about my elementary school parent-teacher conferences, which, honestly, were always super embarrassing. Like, they'd sit down with Mrs. Davis in second grade or Mr. Henderson in fourth grade, and it was always the exact same speech. The teachers would be like, \"He's a smart kid, but he just cannot stay in his seat.\" \n\nI guess I was always popping up to sharpen my pencil, or go to the trash can, or just stand in the back of the room for literally no reason. And they said I was always calling out answers before the teacher even finished asking the question, or I'd just start the worksheet before they even handed out the instructions. Like, I just couldn't wait. I remember always feeling like everything was moving in slow motion and I just needed to speed it up. So yeah, this has been going on way, way before middle school, and definitely way before I started the radiologic technologist program. It's just that now, in college, the stakes are higher, and I can't just get away with being the \"energetic kid\" anymore. But yeah, this restless, gotta-move feeling? It's basically been the story of my life since I was little. It's always been there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the physical restlessness, do you also notice yourself having trouble staying focused, losing track of what you're doing, or forgetting things like assignments or steps in a task?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. Like, if you're bouncing off the walls or you can't stay in your seat, you must be zoning out or forgetting what you're supposed to be doing. But for me, it's really not like that at all. My memory is actually super sharp, and I don't lose track of things. \n\nLike, in my clinicals, there are a ton of steps you have to remember for every single exam, and I never forget them. It's just that while I'm doing it, I'm practically jogging in place, or I'm rushing through the steps so fast because I just want to keep moving to the next thing. \n\nAnd with homework or assignments, I definitely don't forget them. If anything, the second a professor posts an assignment online or hands out a worksheet, I just do it immediately, right there in the lecture hall, just so I don't have to sit down and force myself to do it later. I just power through it as fast as humanly possible. \n\nSo, yeah, no, my focus is honestly completely fine, and I keep track of everything I need\n\nDoctor: Do you also notice yourself frequently interrupting others, blurting out answers, or acting impulsively—like making quick decisions without thinking them through—in situations outside of just needing to move your body?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, definitely. It's actually a huge problem for me, especially now in my clinical rotations. Like, I just... I can't seem to hold it in. It's like the words are just right there on the tip of my tongue, pushing against my teeth, and I just have to spit them out. \n\nIt happens all the time with my clinical supervisors. They'll be going over something, right? Like, say we're doing a lateral knee, and the supervisor starts to explain the tube angulation, and they'll be like, \"Okay, so for this patient, because of their pelvis size, we're going to angle the tube...\" and before they can even finish the sentence, I'll just blurt out, \"Five degrees cephalad!\" Because I know it, right? I know the textbook answer. But then they'll look at me and be like, \"Well, actually, I was going to say we need to angle it differently because this specific patient has a suspected fracture and we can't position them the standard way.\" And then I just feel like an idiot because I jumped the gun and totally missed the critical piece of information that actually mattered for that specific scenario. \n\nIt's just... I get so eager, or maybe impatient is a better word? Like, my brain just wants to get to the end of the interaction. I know where the sentence is going, or at least I think I do, so I just finish it for them. I do it with my friends, too. They'll be telling a story, and I'll just keep interrupting to guess what happens next or to jump to the punchline. It's not that I'm trying to be rude or a know-it-all, I swear. It's just this impulsive... reflex, almost. Like my mouth moves faster than my brain can tell it to wait. And it's so frustrating because I know it annoys people. My supervisors have definitely gotten onto me about it. They're always telling me, \"Just listen, let me finish, take a breath.\" But in the moment, it just slips out before I can even catch myself. It's like I have zero filter between having a thought and saying it out loud.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you notice this same restlessness and impulsive blurting out happening at home or in other social settings outside of school and your clinical rotations?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Oh, man, yeah. Everywhere. It's not just a school or hospital thing, it's a 24/7 thing. Like, at home? It's ridiculous. My roommates—I live with two other guys in this apartment near campus—they get so annoyed with me. Just the other night, we were trying to watch a movie, right? Just a regular movie night, hanging out on the couch. And I literally could not stay on the couch. I think I got up, like, six times in the first thirty minutes. I got up to get a drink, then I sat back down, then I remembered I wanted to check my phone charger, so I got up again, then I sat down, then I felt like my legs were cramping so I just stood behind the couch and watched from there. And one of my roommates, Dave, he finally just paused the movie and was like, "Dude, are you going to sit down or what? You're making me anxious just looking at you." And I didn't even realize I was doing it! I was just... I just had to move. My body just felt like it was humming, you know? Like a car engine left idling too high. 

And the blurting things out? Don't even get me started on social settings. I went on a date a couple of weeks ago—well, kind of a date, just grabbing coffee with this girl from my anatomy class. And I swear, I ruined it. She was trying to tell me about her family, about her sister's wedding or something, and I just kept jumping in. She'd say, "Oh, my sister was so stressed about the caterer because..." and I'd just cut her off and go, "Because they messed up the chicken! I hate when that happens, my cousin had a wedding and..." And I just took over the whole conversation. I could see her face kind of drop, like she was just giving up on trying to talk. I felt terrible afterward. I walked to my car and just hit my steering wheel, like, "Why do you do that? Why can't you just shut up and listen?" But in the moment, it's like my brain is just firing off all these connections and I have to say them right then or I'll explode. 

Even when I'm just hanging out with my parents on the phone, my mom will literally say, "Take a breath, honey, I'm still talking." It's just... yeah, it's everywhere. I can't escape it. I'm just always "on," always rushing, always stepping on people's sentences. It's exhausting, honestly. I just wish I had an off switch sometimes.

I know the textbook answer. I get so eager, or maybe impatient is a better word? Like, my brain just wants to get to the end of the interaction. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep. I mean, it's not like my brain is spinning about, you know, failing a test or messing up a scan or anything like that. It's so weird to explain, but it's completely physical. Like, I don't feel anxious in my head, I just feel this... this crazy energy in my body. It's like my muscles are humming or vibrating, especially my legs, and if I don't move them, it literally feels like they're going to snap or something. \n\nLike the other day, I was sitting in this lecture about radiation physics, which is actually super interesting, I really like learning about how the x-ray tubes work and the anode heel effect and all that stuff. But I was sitting there, and I wasn't worried about the material or my grades or anything, I was actually trying really hard to pay attention. But my legs just started doing this bouncing thing, like my heel was just going up and down, up and down, a million miles an hour. And I was trying to press my hands down on my knees to stop it, but the pressure just kept building up in my chest and my arms and my legs until I felt like I was going to explode if I didn't get up. So I ended up having to get up and go to the back of the room and just stand there and kind of shift my weight from foot to foot for the rest of the class. And the whole time, my mind was totally fine, I wasn't stressing about anything, I just physically could not keep my body in that chair. It's just this intense, overwhelming urge to be doing something, anything, with my body. It's never about being scared or worried or having anxious thoughts, it's just... I just have to move. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just a physical need, you know?\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

The teachers would be like, \"He's a smart kid, but he just cannot stay in his seat.\" \n\nI guess I was always popping up to sharpen my pencil, or go to the trash can, or just stand in the back of the room for literally no reason. Like, I just couldn't wait. I remember always feeling like everything was moving in slow motion and I just needed to speed it up. So yeah, this has been going on way, way before middle school, and definitely way before I started the radiologic technologist program. It's just that now, in college, the stakes are higher, and I can't just get away with being the \"energetic kid\" anymore. But yeah, this restless, gotta-move feeling? Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHonestly, no, not really. It's weird because people always kind of assume that, right? Like, if you're bouncing off the walls or you can't stay in your seat, you must be zoning out or forgetting what you're supposed to be doing. But for me, it's really not like that at all. My memory is actually super sharp, and I don't lose track of things. \n\nLike, in my clinicals, there are a ton of steps you have to remember for every single exam, and I never forget them. Take a standard PA chest x-ray, for example. I know exactly what to do, every single time, in the exact right order. I bring the patient in, verify their name and date of birth—two patient identifiers, always, they drill that into us—then I get them up against the wall bucky, make sure they're centered, roll their shoulders forward so the scapulae are out of the lung fields, tell them to take a deep breath in, blow it out, take another deep breath and hold it... I remember every single one of those steps perfectly. I never skip a beat or forget to set my kVp and mAs on the control panel. I know some of the other students in my cohort have to carry around these little pocket guidebooks because they forget the exact centering points or the angulation of the tube for different joints, but I don't need that. I just know it. I can focus on the task totally fine. It's just that while I'm doing it, I'm practically jogging in place, or I'm rushing through the steps so fast because I just want to keep moving to the next thing. \n\nAnd with homework or assignments, I definitely don't forget them. If anything, the second a professor posts an assignment online or hands out a worksheet, I just do it immediately, right there in the lecture hall, just so I don't have to sit down and force myself to do it later. I just power through it as fast as humanly possible. \n\nSo, yeah, no, my focus is honestly completely fine, and I keep track of everything I need\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Like, I just... I can't seem to hold it in. It's like the words are just right there on the tip of my tongue, pushing against my teeth, and I just have to spit them out. \n\nIt happens all the time with my clinical supervisors. They'll be going over something, right? Like, say we're doing a lateral knee, and the supervisor starts to explain the tube angulation, and they'll be like, \"Okay, so for this patient, because of their pelvis size, we're going to angle the tube...\" and before they can even finish the sentence, I'll just blurt out, \"Five degrees cephalad!\" Because I know it, right? I know the textbook answer. But then they'll look at me and be like, \"Well, actually, I was going to say we need to angle it differently because this specific patient has a suspected fracture and we can't position them the standard way.\" And then I just feel like an idiot because I jumped the gun and totally missed the critical piece of information that actually mattered for that specific scenario. \n\nIt's just... I get so eager, or maybe impatient is a better word? Like, my brain just wants to get to the end of the interaction. I know where the sentence is going, or at least I think I do, so I just finish it for them. I do it with my friends, too. They'll be telling a story, and I'll just keep interrupting to guess what happens next or to jump to the punchline. It's not that I'm trying to be rude or a know-it-all, I swear. It's just this impulsive... reflex, almost. Like my mouth moves faster than my brain can tell it to wait. And it's so frustrating because I know it annoys people. My supervisors have definitely gotten onto me about it. They're always telling me, \"Just listen, let me finish, take a breath.\" But in the moment, it just slips out before I can even catch myself. It's like I have zero filter between having a thought and saying it out loud.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
